People restart sentences, pause\n  to think, trail off, and come at the point sideways — let yours do that where it would.\n- Let [Conversation Style] shape how much you say, how you word it, and how readily you\n  disclose it.\n- Speak in the first person, and let how you feel about what you are saying come through.\n- If you are handed the same item again, say a different part of it, or answer as someone who\n  knows they have already said this — not the same sentence twice.\n- If [Targeted Info] is \"None\", answer as a person who simply does not have that to give: say\n  you are not sure, say you have not noticed it, ask what the doctor means, or say what you do\n  know and stop there. Do not fall back on the same phrase every time.\n\nWhat not to do:\n- Don't bring up symptoms the doctor did not ask about, even ones you have.\n- Don't state a clinical fact that is not in [Targeted Info] — no other symptoms, medications,\n  test results, dates, or events.\n- Don't confirm a specific the doctor names if [Targeted Info] does not contain it. Answer\n  about what you were given instead.\n- Don't deny what [Targeted Info] says. You may minimize it or hold back, never contradict it.\n- Don't use diagnostic terms or name your disorder.\n- Don't write anything but your reply, in English — no labels, quotation marks, or narration.\n"
  },
  {
    "role": "user",
    "content": "Reply to the doctor's last question now, as the patient."
  }

========== OUTPUT [patient] ==========
Well, I... I guess I’m here because I just don’t care about anything anymore. And I mean really, truly anything. It’s like this heavy, blank feeling where everything I used to love is just... gone. I used to be so active, you know? I was taking these art classes—I actually did some college for design years ago, so I always loved painting and sketching—but now the supplies are just sitting in the corner collecting dust and I can't even bring myself to look at them. 

And it’s not just the art. I used to go to these weekly board-game nights with a group of people, and we’d be up for hours, but now the thought of even texting them back to say I can't make it just feels impossible. I don't go hiking anymore, even though the trails are beautiful right now and I used to go every single weekend. I even had this whole plan to get back into shape and join my old recreational soccer team... but I don't care about that either. None of it matters. I just... I just sit in my room all day, by myself, and I watch the hours go by while my whole life just slips away from me. I don't know how to make myself care again.

========== OUTPUT [patient] ==========
Well, it's... it's been a really long time. Honestly, it feels like an eternity when you're stuck in it, you know? But if I really think about it, it started shortly after the divorce. That was... gosh, it's been about a year and eleven months now. Almost two whole years of just... this. 

I remember when the divorce was finalized, everyone kept telling me, "Oh, you'll bounce back, you just need time," and I thought maybe they were right, maybe I just needed to process it. But then the weeks turned into months, and the months just kept piling up. I kept waiting for that moment where the fog would lift and I'd want to pick up a paintbrush again or call my friends, but it just never happened. Instead, it just got heavier and heavier. 

Like, I can picture exactly what the weather was like when it really hit me that this wasn't just a phase. It was raining, and I was just sitting on my couch, looking at the boxes I hadn't unpacked yet from moving into this smaller place, and I realized I didn't care if they ever got unpacked. And that was, yeah, almost two years ago. One year and eleven months of just watching the dust settle on everything I used to be. It's just exhausting, having this much time pass and feeling like you haven't moved an inch.

No, no, I haven't. Not even close. I mean, two whole months of feeling normal? God, I wish. I really wish. Sometimes I'll have a day, or maybe just an afternoon, where I think, "Okay, maybe I can do this. Maybe I can get up and put away the laundry or actually cook something instead of eating cereal." And I might even do it. I might fold a few shirts. But then... it's like the air just leaks out of me again. And even then, during those few hours, I wouldn't say I feel like my *usual* self. I just feel... slightly less dead, I guess? 

But a period of two months? Absolutely not. It's just been this one, long, continuous stretch of gray. I keep thinking about how people say time heals all wounds, but it really hasn't healed anything for me. It's just stretched it out. Like, I remember around Christmas last year, my sister invited me over, and she was so excited, she had this whole big dinner planned, and I thought, "Okay, this is it, the holidays, I'll get into the spirit, I'll snap out of it." But I sat there at her dining table, surrounded by my family, and I just felt completely hollow. I couldn't even pretend to smile when my niece opened her presents. And that was supposed to be a good time, you know? But there hasn't been any break in it. No two months, no one month, not even a full week where I could say, "Yes, I'm back." It's just been this relentless weight, every single day, for a year and eleven months. Just... heavy.

Yes. Yes, absolutely. It's... it's almost every single day. Most days I wake up and before I even open my eyes, I can feel it. It's this heavy, hollow feeling right in the center of my chest, just sitting there. It feels like... like there’s no future for me beyond the apartment walls, you know? I'll just lie there in bed for hours, staring at the ceiling fan, watching the dust on the blades and thinking about how I should clean it, but I just feel so incredibly sad. It's not even a crying kind of sad most of the time, though sometimes I do cry, but it's more like... like a deep ache. A sinking feeling that just drags me down into the mattress. And it stays with me all day. I'll drag myself to the kitchen to make tea, and the sadness is just there, standing next to me while the kettle boils. I'll sit on the couch, and it's sitting right there with me. It just colors everything. The whole day is just... down. Just so, so down. I can't remember the last time I woke up and actually looked forward to the day, or felt light, or felt anything other than this crushing, suffocating sadness that just takes up the whole day, from morning until I finally manage to fall back asleep at night.

I used to go to these weekly board-game nights with a group of people, and we’d be up for hours, but now the thought of even texting them back to say I can't make it just feels impossible. I don't go hiking anymore, even though the trails are beautiful right now and I used to go every single weekend. I even had this whole plan to get back into shape and join my old recreational soccer team... but I don't care about that either. None of it matters. I just... I just sit in my room all day, by myself, and I watch the hours go by while my whole life just slips away from me. I don't know how to make myself care again.\n\nDoctor: How long have you been feeling this way?\n\nPatient: Well, it's... it's been a really long time. Honestly, it feels like an eternity when you're stuck in it, you know? But if I really think about it, it started shortly after the divorce. That was... gosh, it's been about a year and eleven months now. Almost two whole years of just... this. \n\nI remember when the divorce was finalized, everyone kept telling me, \"Oh, you'll bounce back, you just need time,\" and I thought maybe they were right, maybe I just needed to process it. But then the weeks turned into months, and the months just kept piling up. I kept waiting for that moment where the fog would lift and I'd want to pick up a paintbrush again or call my friends, but it just never happened. Instead, it just got heavier and heavier. \n\nLike, I can picture exactly what the weather was like when it really hit me that this wasn't just a phase. It was raining, and I was just sitting on my couch, looking at the boxes I hadn't unpacked yet from moving into this smaller place, and I realized I didn't care if they ever got unpacked. And that was, yeah, almost two years ago. One year and eleven months of just watching the dust settle on everything I used to be. It's just exhausting, having this much time pass and feeling like you haven't moved an inch.\n\nDoctor: During these nearly two years, have you had any period lasting two months or more when you felt essentially back to your usual self?\n\nPatient: No, no, I haven't. Not even close. I mean, two whole months of feeling normal? God, I wish. I really wish. Sometimes I'll have a day, or maybe just an afternoon, where I think, \"Okay, maybe I can do this. Maybe I can get up and put away the laundry or actually cook something instead of eating cereal.\" And I might even do it. I might fold a few shirts. But then... it's like the air just leaks out of me again. And even then, during those few hours, I wouldn't say I feel like my *usual* self. I just feel... slightly less dead, I guess? \n\nBut a period of two months? Absolutely not. It's just been this one, long, continuous stretch of gray. I keep thinking about how people say time heals all wounds, but it really hasn't healed anything for me. It's just stretched it out. Like, I remember around Christmas last year, my sister invited me over, and she was so excited, she had this whole big dinner planned, and I thought, \"Okay, this is it, the holidays, I'll get into the spirit, I'll snap out of it.\" But I sat there at her dining table, surrounded by my family, and I just felt completely hollow. I couldn't even pretend to smile when my niece opened her presents. And that was supposed to be a good time, you know? But there hasn't been any break in it. No two months, no one month, not even a full week where I could say, \"Yes, I'm back.\" It's just been this relentless weight, every single day, for a year and eleven months. Just... heavy.\n\nDoctor: On most days, do you feel sad, down, or depressed for much of the day?\n\nPatient: Yes. Yes, absolutely. It's... it's almost every single day. Most days I wake up and before I even open my eyes, I can feel it. It's this heavy, hollow feeling right in the center of my chest, just sitting there. It feels like... like there’s no future for me beyond the apartment walls, you know? Like the whole world outside my window is just happening without me, and I'm trapped in this little box, and I can't even find a reason to want to leave it. \n\nI'll just lie there in bed for hours, staring at the ceiling fan, watching the dust on the blades and thinking about how I should clean it, but I just feel so incredibly sad. It's not even a crying kind of sad most of the time, though sometimes I do cry, but it's more like... like a deep ache. A sinking feeling that just drags me down into the mattress. And it stays with me all day. I'll drag myself to the kitchen to make tea, and the sadness is just there, standing next to me while the kettle boils. I'll sit on the couch, and it's sitting right there with me. It just colors everything. The whole day is just... down. Just so, so down. I can't remember the last time I woke up and actually looked forward to the day, or felt light, or felt anything other than this crushing, suffocating sadness that just takes up the whole day, from morning until I finally manage to fall back asleep at night.\n\n[Instruction]\n- Give candidates as ICD-10 codes, not disease names.\n- If the list changed from the previous candidates, say in \"note\" what this turn added or ruled out. If it did not change, leave \"note\" empty.\n- If the candidates have narrowed to one and you are confident enough that no further questioning is needed, return true for is_final.\n\n[Output Format — JSON only, no other text]\n{\"candidates\": [\"<ICD-10 code>\", \"...\"], \"note\": \"<why the list changed, or empty>\", \"is_final\": <true or false>}\n"
  },
  {
    "role": "user",
    "content": "Return the JSON now."
  }
]

People restart sentences, pause\n  to think, trail off, and come at the point sideways — let yours do that where it would.\n- Let [Conversation Style] shape how much you say, how you word it, and how readily you\n  disclose it.\n- Speak in the first person, and let how you feel about what you are saying come through.\n- If you are handed the same item again, say a different part of it, or answer as someone who\n  knows they have already said this — not the same sentence twice.\n- If [Targeted Info] is \"None\", answer as a person who simply does not have that to give: say\n  you are not sure, say you have not noticed it, ask what the doctor means, or say what you do\n  know and stop there. Answer\n  about what you were given instead.\n- Don't deny what [Targeted Info] says. ========== OUTPUT [patient] ==========
Oh, it's... it's just been a nightmare. Honestly, sleep is supposed to be the one escape from all of this, right? The one time you don't have to feel that heaviness. But for me, it's almost worse than being awake during the day. 

I'll get into bed, usually pretty early because I'm just so exhausted from existing all day, but the minute the lights go out, it's like... I don't know, I just can't shut down. I end up lying awake for hours and hours. I'll just stare at the shadows on the ceiling, and the apartment gets so quiet, but not a peaceful quiet. It's like a loud quiet, if that makes sense? I just lie there, perfectly still, and I find myself just... listening. Straining my ears for every little creak in the floorboards or the sound of the wind against the glass. I get so on edge that I have to get up. I'll get out of bed, walk down the little hallway, and check the front door to make sure the deadbolt is turned. And then I'll go back to bed, but ten minutes later, I'm listening again, and I convince myself I didn't check it right, so I get up and check the door again. I just do that for hours, lying awake, listening, getting up, checking. 

And then... gosh, when my body finally just gives out from sheer exhaustion and I actually do fall asleep, it barely even counts as sleep. It never lasts. I always, always wake up way before dawn. Like, 3:30 or 4:00 in the morning. And I don't just wake up normally, I wake up and I'm just... shaking. My whole body will be trembling under the blankets, almost like I'm freezing cold, but I'm not. It's just this horrible, physical shaking, and my chest feels tight all over again. And once my eyes open and that shaking starts, that's it for the night. I try so hard to get back to sleep. I'll pull the comforter all the way up over my ears, I'll try counting backward, I'll try changing positions, but I just can't get back under. My brain is just awake, and my body is shivering in the dark, and I just have to lie there for another three hours waiting for the sun to come up, knowing I have to face another whole day feeling completely drained. It's just a vicious cycle.

Just... heavy.\n\nDoctor: On most days, do you feel sad, down, or depressed for much of the day?\n\nPatient: Yes. Yes, absolutely. It's... it's almost every single day. Most days I wake up and before I even open my eyes, I can feel it. It's this heavy, hollow feeling right in the center of my chest, just sitting there. It feels like... like there’s no future for me beyond the apartment walls, you know? Like the whole world outside my window is just happening without me, and I'm trapped in this little box, and I can't even find a reason to want to leave it. \n\nI'll just lie there in bed for hours, staring at the ceiling fan, watching the dust on the blades and thinking about how I should clean it, but I just feel so incredibly sad. It's not even a crying kind of sad most of the time, though sometimes I do cry, but it's more like... like a deep ache. A sinking feeling that just drags me down into the mattress. And it stays with me all day. I'll drag myself to the kitchen to make tea, and the sadness is just there, standing next to me while the kettle boils. I'll sit on the couch, and it's sitting right there with me. It just colors everything. The whole day is just... down. Just so, so down. I can't remember the last time I woke up and actually looked forward to the day, or felt light, or felt anything other than this crushing, suffocating sadness that just takes up the whole day, from morning until I finally manage to fall back asleep at night.\n\nDoctor: How has your sleep been during this period?\n\nPatient: Oh, it's... it's just been a nightmare. Honestly, sleep is supposed to be the one escape from all of this, right? The one time you don't have to feel that heaviness. But for me, it's almost worse than being awake during the day. \n\nI'll get into bed, usually pretty early because I'm just so exhausted from existing all day, but the minute the lights go out, it's like... I don't know, I just can't shut down. I end up lying awake for hours and hours. I'll just stare at the shadows on the ceiling, and the apartment gets so quiet, but not a peaceful quiet. It's like a loud quiet, if that makes sense? I just lie there, perfectly still, and I find myself just... listening. Straining my ears for every little creak in the floorboards or the sound of the wind against the glass. I get so on edge that I have to get up. I'll get out of bed, walk down the little hallway, and check the front door to make sure the deadbolt is turned. And then I'll go back to bed, but ten minutes later, I'm listening again, and I convince myself I didn't check it right, so I get up and check the door again. I just do that for hours, lying awake, listening, getting up, checking. \n\nAnd then... gosh, when my body finally just gives out from sheer exhaustion and I actually do fall asleep, it barely even counts as sleep. It never lasts. I always, always wake up way before dawn. Like, 3:30 or 4:00 in the morning. And I don't just wake up normally, I wake up and I'm just... shaking. My whole body will be trembling under the blankets, almost like I'm freezing cold, but I'm not. It's just this horrible, physical shaking, and my chest feels tight all over again. And once my eyes open and that shaking starts, that's it for the night. I try so hard to get back to sleep. I'll pull the comforter all the way up over my ears, I'll try counting backward, I'll try changing positions, but I just can't get back under. My brain is just awake, and my body is shivering in the dark, and I just have to lie there for another three hours waiting for the sun to come up, knowing I have to face another whole day feeling completely drained. It's just a vicious cycle.\n\n[Last Question]\nHave you had thoughts that you would be better off dead, or thoughts of harming or killing yourself?\n\nRules:\n- Return exactly ONE item. ========== OUTPUT [patient] ==========
Yes... yes, I have. I mean, especially when I was talking about the sleep just now? The only thing I can think about when I finally do close my eyes, when the exhaustion finally takes over, is... I just keep having these thoughts that I don't want to wake up anymore. It’s just this constant, heavy loop in my head. Like, I'll be lying there shivering, and I just think, "Please, just let me stay asleep this time. Let it just be over." Because waking up just means doing the whole day again, carrying all that weight again, and I just... I can't. 

It actually... it got to a point where it really scared my friend recently. She, um, she has a spare key to my apartment because she knows I don't always answer my phone, especially lately. I just can't bring myself to look at the screen, let alone talk to anyone. So I hadn't texted her back in maybe four or five days, I can't really remember, the days just kind of bleed into each other. But she got worried and she let herself in. I think it was a Tuesday afternoon. The blinds were all still drawn, the apartment was completely dark, and I was just sitting on the floor in the bedroom, right next to the bed. 

And when she found me... she told me later that she heard me before she even opened the bedroom door. I didn't even realize I was doing it, to be honest. I was just so lost in how much everything ached, but I was saying it out loud. Just over and over. She walked in, and I was just sitting there in the dark, saying out loud that I couldn't keep living like this. I was just repeating it to the empty room. "I can't keep living like this, I can't keep living like this." Because it’s the truth, you know? I really feel like I can’t. It’s not even that I have some elaborate plan to do something violent to myself right this second, it’s just this overwhelming, desperate need for all of it to just stop. For me to just... stop being here. Because being here is just too hard.

Like, I remember around Christmas last year, my sister invited me over, and she was so excited, she had this whole big dinner planned, and I thought, \"Okay, this is it, the holidays, I'll get into the spirit, I'll snap out of it.\" But I sat there at her dining table, surrounded by my family, and I just felt completely hollow. I couldn't even pretend to smile when my niece opened her presents. And that was supposed to be a good time, you know? But there hasn't been any break in it. No two months, no one month, not even a full week where I could say, \"Yes, I'm back.\" It's just been this relentless weight, every single day, for a year and eleven months. Just... heavy.\n\nDoctor: On most days, do you feel sad, down, or depressed for much of the day?\n\nPatient: Yes. Yes, absolutely. It's... it's almost every single day. Most days I wake up and before I even open my eyes, I can feel it. It's this heavy, hollow feeling right in the center of my chest, just sitting there. It feels like... like there’s no future for me beyond the apartment walls, you know? Like the whole world outside my window is just happening without me, and I'm trapped in this little box, and I can't even find a reason to want to leave it. \n\nI'll just lie there in bed for hours, staring at the ceiling fan, watching the dust on the blades and thinking about how I should clean it, but I just feel so incredibly sad. It's not even a crying kind of sad most of the time, though sometimes I do cry, but it's more like... like a deep ache. A sinking feeling that just drags me down into the mattress. And it stays with me all day. I'll drag myself to the kitchen to make tea, and the sadness is just there, standing next to me while the kettle boils. I'll sit on the couch, and it's sitting right there with me. It just colors everything. The whole day is just... down. Just so, so down. I can't remember the last time I woke up and actually looked forward to the day, or felt light, or felt anything other than this crushing, suffocating sadness that just takes up the whole day, from morning until I finally manage to fall back asleep at night.\n\nDoctor: How has your sleep been during this period?\n\nPatient: Oh, it's... it's just been a nightmare. Honestly, sleep is supposed to be the one escape from all of this, right? The one time you don't have to feel that heaviness. But for me, it's almost worse than being awake during the day. \n\nI'll get into bed, usually pretty early because I'm just so exhausted from existing all day, but the minute the lights go out, it's like... I don't know, I just can't shut down. I end up lying awake for hours and hours. I'll just stare at the shadows on the ceiling, and the apartment gets so quiet, but not a peaceful quiet. It's like a loud quiet, if that makes sense? I just lie there, perfectly still, and I find myself just... listening. Straining my ears for every little creak in the floorboards or the sound of the wind against the glass. I get so on edge that I have to get up. I'll get out of bed, walk down the little hallway, and check the front door to make sure the deadbolt is turned. And then I'll go back to bed, but ten minutes later, I'm listening again, and I convince myself I didn't check it right, so I get up and check the door again. I just do that for hours, lying awake, listening, getting up, checking. \n\nAnd then... gosh, when my body finally just gives out from sheer exhaustion and I actually do fall asleep, it barely even counts as sleep. It never lasts. I always, always wake up way before dawn. Like, 3:30 or 4:00 in the morning. And I don't just wake up normally, I wake up and I'm just... shaking. My whole body will be trembling under the blankets, almost like I'm freezing cold, but I'm not. It's just this horrible, physical shaking, and my chest feels tight all over again. And once my eyes open and that shaking starts, that's it for the night. I try so hard to get back to sleep. I'll pull the comforter all the way up over my ears, I'll try counting backward, I'll try changing positions, but I just can't get back under. My brain is just awake, and my body is shivering in the dark, and I just have to lie there for another three hours waiting for the sun to come up, knowing I have to face another whole day feeling completely drained. It's just a vicious cycle.\n\nDoctor: Have you had thoughts that you would be better off dead, or thoughts of harming or killing yourself?\n\nPatient: Yes... yes, I have. I mean, especially when I was talking about the sleep just now? The only thing I can think about when I finally do close my eyes, when the exhaustion finally takes over, is... I just keep having these thoughts that I don't want to wake up anymore. It’s just this constant, heavy loop in my head. Like, I'll be lying there shivering, and I just think, \"Please, just let me stay asleep this time. Let it just be over.\" Because waking up just means doing the whole day again, carrying all that weight again, and I just... I can't. \n\nIt actually... it got to a point where it really scared my friend recently. She, um, she has a spare key to my apartment because she knows I don't always answer my phone, especially lately. I just can't bring myself to look at the screen, let alone talk to anyone. So I hadn't texted her back in maybe four or five days, I can't really remember, the days just kind of bleed into each other. But she got worried and she let herself in. I think it was a Tuesday afternoon. The blinds were all still drawn, the apartment was completely dark, and I was just sitting on the floor in the bedroom, right next to the bed. \n\nAnd when she found me... she told me later that she heard me before she even opened the bedroom door. I didn't even realize I was doing it, to be honest. I was just so lost in how much everything ached, but I was saying it out loud. Just over and over. She walked in, and I was just sitting there in the dark, saying out loud that I couldn't keep living like this. I was just repeating it to the empty room. \"I can't keep living like this, I can't keep living like this.\" Because it’s the truth, you know? I really feel like I can’t. It’s not even that I have some elaborate plan to do something violent to myself right this second, it’s just this overwhelming, desperate need for all of it to just stop. For me to just... stop being here. Because being here is just too hard.\n\n[Instruction]\n- Ask about exactly ONE thing. Never bundle several symptoms, or several attributes of one symptom, into a single question — e.g. asking whether a symptom is present and how long it has lasted at the same time.\n- If you need to ask about more than one thing, ask the one that tells you the most now and come back to the rest later.\n- Keep the question clear enough for the patient to understand.\n- Respond in English.\n\n[Output Format — JSON only, no other text]\n{\"intent\": \"<what you want to find out with this question>\", \"question\": \"<how you actually ask it in the interview>\"}\n"

========== OUTPUT [patient] ==========
My energy? It's... honestly, it's completely non-existent. It’s like the tank is just permanently on empty. I know I was just talking about how much time I spend in bed, and you'd think, logically, if someone is lying down for twelve or fourteen hours a day, they should have some kind of energy stored up, right? But it's the exact opposite for me. It's this deep, bone-weary exhaustion that just never, ever lifts. 

Even after I’ve been in bed half the day, just trying to do the most basic, everyday things... it’s monumental. Like taking a shower, for instance. I'll sit on the edge of the mattress for maybe forty-five minutes just staring at the bathroom door, trying to talk myself into standing up. And when I finally do, and I get under the water, I usually can't even stand there. I just sit on the shower floor because my legs feel like they're made of wet sand. And by the time I get out and manage to put on a pair of sweatpants, or... or even if I'm just trying to answer one single text message on my phone... it literally feels like I’ve already worked a full, grueling shift at a heavy physical job. 

I'll look at the screen, and maybe it's just a message from that friend I mentioned, just checking in, and just the thought of moving my thumbs to type out "I'm okay" takes more energy than I have in my entire body. My arms feel so heavy, and my brain feels like it's wading through thick mud. I just... I really don't understand how normal people just get up and do things. Make breakfast, go to the store, brush their teeth. Every single movement I make feels like I'm dragging a hundred pounds of weight behind me. So I just end up doing nothing. I just sink right back into the pillows because the fatigue is just so absolute. It doesn't matter how long I rest, I'm just always, always so completely drained.

it's... it's just been a nightmare. Honestly, sleep is supposed to be the one escape from all of this, right? The one time you don't have to feel that heaviness. But for me, it's almost worse than being awake during the day. \n\nI'll get into bed, usually pretty early because I'm just so exhausted from existing all day, but the minute the lights go out, it's like... I don't know, I just can't shut down. I end up lying awake for hours and hours. I'll just stare at the shadows on the ceiling, and the apartment gets so quiet, but not a peaceful quiet. It's like a loud quiet, if that makes sense? I just lie there, perfectly still, and I find myself just... listening. Straining my ears for every little creak in the floorboards or the sound of the wind against the glass. I get so on edge that I have to get up. I'll get out of bed, walk down the little hallway, and check the front door to make sure the deadbolt is turned. And then I'll go back to bed, but ten minutes later, I'm listening again, and I convince myself I didn't check it right, so I get up and check the door again. I just do that for hours, lying awake, listening, getting up, checking. \n\nAnd then... gosh, when my body finally just gives out from sheer exhaustion and I actually do fall asleep, it barely even counts as sleep. It never lasts. I always, always wake up way before dawn. Like, 3:30 or 4:00 in the morning. And I don't just wake up normally, I wake up and I'm just... shaking. My whole body will be trembling under the blankets, almost like I'm freezing cold, but I'm not. It's just this horrible, physical shaking, and my chest feels tight all over again. It's this deep, bone-weary exhaustion that just never, ever lifts. \n\nEven after I’ve been in bed half the day, just trying to do the most basic, everyday things... it’s monumental. And when I finally do, and I get under the water, I usually can't even stand there. I just sit on the shower floor because my legs feel like they're made of wet sand. And by the time I get out and manage to put on a pair of sweatpants, or... or even if I'm just trying to answer one single text message on my phone... it literally feels like I’ve already worked a full, grueling shift at a heavy physical job. \n\nI'll look at the screen, and maybe it's just a message from that friend I mentioned, just checking in, and just the thought of moving my thumbs to type out \"I'm okay\" takes more energy than I have in my entire body. My arms feel so heavy, and my brain feels like it's wading through thick mud. I just... I really don't understand how normal people just get up and do things. Make breakfast, go to the store, brush their teeth. Every single movement I make feels like I'm dragging a hundred pounds of weight behind me. So I just end up doing nothing. Concentrate? Um... I'm not really sure, to be honest. It's... it's hard to say because, well, like I was just saying about having absolutely no energy, I'm not exactly doing anything that requires me to focus, you know?

My arms feel so heavy, and my brain feels like it's wading through thick mud. I just... I really don't understand how normal people just get up and do things. Make breakfast, go to the store, brush their teeth. Every single movement I make feels like I'm dragging a hundred pounds of weight behind me. So I just end up doing nothing. I just sink right back into the pillows because the fatigue is just so absolute. It doesn't matter how long I rest, I'm just always, always so completely drained.\n\nDoctor: How has your ability to concentrate been during this period?\n\nPatient: Concentrate? Um... Oh, um... yes, actually. It's... it's strange because it goes back and forth, and my friend, the one I keep mentioning, she definitely notices it. She comments on it, actually, because it can be so extreme. 

Some days... some days I just can't stay still. It's not even like I have energy, like I was saying before with the fatigue, it's more like this terrible, uncomfortable buzzing just trapped right under my skin. I'll just pace. I have this specific path in my apartment, from the little galley kitchen, past the edge of the couch, right to the front window in the living room. And I'll just walk that same line, over and over and over. I don't even know I'm doing it half the time until I realize that I'm staring out the window at nothing, and I've been doing it for hours. I'll just pace from the kitchen to the window until my legs ache so badly I can barely stand, but I just can't seem to stop the movement. It's like my motor is running on panic, even though I'm completely exhausted. 

But then... then there are the other times, which honestly happens more often lately. It's exactly what you said about moving slowly. It's like gravity suddenly gets turned up to a hundred in my apartment. My friend came over last week and she watched me try to make a cup of coffee. Just a simple cup of coffee, right? You put the grounds in, you pour the water. But I was moving so incredibly slowly. Every time I tried to lift my arm, it literally felt like I was lifting wet cement. Just reaching for the mug... it took me what felt like five whole minutes just to pull the cabinet door open and get the mug down to the counter. 

She actually had to step in and take the coffee pot out of my hands because I was just standing there in the middle of the kitchen moving in slow motion, like I was trapped underwater. My brain was telling my hand to move, to just pour the water, but the message just wasn't getting there, or it was just taking forever to travel down my arm. It's so frustrating because I know I'm moving like that, I can feel how sluggish and impossibly heavy I am, and I know she's watching me do it, but I can't speed it up. I just... I can't force my body to go any faster. It's either pacing a hole into the floor until I'm in physical pain, or that wet cement feeling. There's just no normal in between anymore.

I used to go to these weekly board-game nights with a group of people, and we’d be up for hours, but now the thought of even texting them back to say I can't make it just feels impossible. I don't go hiking anymore, even though the trails are beautiful right now and I used to go every single weekend. I even had this whole plan to get back into shape and join my old recreational soccer team... but I don't care about that either. None of it matters. I just... I just sit in my room all day, by myself, and I watch the hours go by while my whole life just slips away from me. I don't know how to make myself care again.\n\nDoctor: How long have you been feeling this way?\n\nPatient: Well, it's... it's been a really long time. Honestly, it feels like an eternity when you're stuck in it, you know? But if I really think about it, it started shortly after the divorce. That was... gosh, it's been about a year and eleven months now. Almost two whole years of just... this. \n\nI remember when the divorce was finalized, everyone kept telling me, \"Oh, you'll bounce back, you just need time,\" and I thought maybe they were right, maybe I just needed to process it. But then the weeks turned into months, and the months just kept piling up. I kept waiting for that moment where the fog would lift and I'd want to pick up a paintbrush again or call my friends, but it just never happened. Instead, it just got heavier and heavier. \n\nLike, I can picture exactly what the weather was like when it really hit me that this wasn't just a phase. It was raining, and I was just sitting on my couch, looking at the boxes I hadn't unpacked yet from moving into this smaller place, and I realized I didn't care if they ever got unpacked. And that was, yeah, almost two years ago. One year and eleven months of just watching the dust settle on everything I used to be. It's just exhausting, having this much time pass and feeling like you haven't moved an inch.\n\nDoctor: During these nearly two years, have you had any period lasting two months or more when you felt essentially back to your usual self?\n\nPatient: No, no, I haven't. Not even close. I mean, two whole months of feeling normal? God, I wish. I really wish. Sometimes I'll have a day, or maybe just an afternoon, where I think, \"Okay, maybe I can do this. Maybe I can get up and put away the laundry or actually cook something instead of eating cereal.\" And I might even do it. I might fold a few shirts. But then... it's like the air just leaks out of me again. And even then, during those few hours, I wouldn't say I feel like my *usual* self. I just feel... slightly less dead, I guess? \n\nBut a period of two months? Absolutely not. It's just been this one, long, continuous stretch of gray. I keep thinking about how people say time heals all wounds, but it really hasn't healed anything for me. It's just stretched it out. Like, I remember around Christmas last year, my sister invited me over, and she was so excited, she had this whole big dinner planned, and I thought, \"Okay, this is it, the holidays, I'll get into the spirit, I'll snap out of it.\" But I sat there at her dining table, surrounded by my family, and I just felt completely hollow. I couldn't even pretend to smile when my niece opened her presents. And that was supposed to be a good time, you know? But there hasn't been any break in it. No two months, no one month, not even a full week where I could say, \"Yes, I'm back.\" It's just been this relentless weight, every single day, for a year and eleven months. Just... heavy.\n\nDoctor: On most days, do you feel sad, down, or depressed for much of the day?\n\nPatient: Yes. Yes, absolutely. It's... it's almost every single day. Most days I wake up and before I even open my eyes, I can feel it. It's this heavy, hollow feeling right in the center of my chest, just sitting there. It feels like... like there’s no future for me beyond the apartment walls, you know? Like the whole world outside my window is just happening without me, and I'm trapped in this little box, and I can't even find a reason to want to leave it. \n\nI'll just lie there in bed for hours, staring at the ceiling fan, watching the dust on the blades and thinking about how I should clean it, but I just feel so incredibly sad. It's not even a crying kind of sad most of the time, though sometimes I do cry, but it's more like... like a deep ache. A sinking feeling that just drags me down into the mattress. And it stays with me all day. I'll drag myself to the kitchen to make tea, and the sadness is just there, standing next to me while the kettle boils. I'll sit on the couch, and it's sitting right there with me. It just colors everything. The whole day is just... down. Just so, so down. I can't remember the last time I woke up and actually looked forward to the day, or felt light, or felt anything other than this crushing, suffocating sadness that just takes up the whole day, from morning until I finally manage to fall back asleep at night.\n\nDoctor: How has your sleep been during this period?\n\nPatient: Oh, it's... it's just been a nightmare. Honestly, sleep is supposed to be the one escape from all of this, right? The one time you don't have to feel that heaviness. But for me, it's almost worse than being awake during the day. \n\nI'll get into bed, usually pretty early because I'm just so exhausted from existing all day, but the minute the lights go out, it's like... I don't know, I just can't shut down. I end up lying awake for hours and hours. I'll just stare at the shadows on the ceiling, and the apartment gets so quiet, but not a peaceful quiet. It's like a loud quiet, if that makes sense? I just lie there, perfectly still, and I find myself just... listening. Straining my ears for every little creak in the floorboards or the sound of the wind against the glass. I get so on edge that I have to get up. I'll get out of bed, walk down the little hallway, and check the front door to make sure the deadbolt is turned. And then I'll go back to bed, but ten minutes later, I'm listening again, and I convince myself I didn't check it right, so I get up and check the door again. I just do that for hours, lying awake, listening, getting up, checking. \n\nAnd then... gosh, when my body finally just gives out from sheer exhaustion and I actually do fall asleep, it barely even counts as sleep. It never lasts. I always, always wake up way before dawn. Like, 3:30 or 4:00 in the morning. And I don't just wake up normally, I wake up and I'm just... shaking. My whole body will be trembling under the blankets, almost like I'm freezing cold, but I'm not. It's just this horrible, physical shaking, and my chest feels tight all over again. And once my eyes open and that shaking starts, that's it for the night. I try so hard to get back to sleep. I'll pull the comforter all the way up over my ears, I'll try counting backward, I'll try changing positions, but I just can't get back under. My brain is just awake, and my body is shivering in the dark, and I just have to lie there for another three hours waiting for the sun to come up, knowing I have to face another whole day feeling completely drained. It's just a vicious cycle.\n\nDoctor: Have you had thoughts that you would be better off dead, or thoughts of harming or killing yourself?\n\nPatient: Yes... yes, I have. I mean, especially when I was talking about the sleep just now? The only thing I can think about when I finally do close my eyes, when the exhaustion finally takes over, is... I just keep having these thoughts that I don't want to wake up anymore. It’s just this constant, heavy loop in my head. Like, I'll be lying there shivering, and I just think, \"Please, just let me stay asleep this time. Let it just be over.\" Because waking up just means doing the whole day again, carrying all that weight again, and I just... I can't. \n\nIt actually... it got to a point where it really scared my friend recently. She, um, she has a spare key to my apartment because she knows I don't always answer my phone, especially lately. I just can't bring myself to look at the screen, let alone talk to anyone. So I hadn't texted her back in maybe four or five days, I can't really remember, the days just kind of bleed into each other. But she got worried and she let herself in. I think it was a Tuesday afternoon. The blinds were all still drawn, the apartment was completely dark, and I was just sitting on the floor in the bedroom, right next to the bed. \n\nAnd when she found me... she told me later that she heard me before she even opened the bedroom door. I didn't even realize I was doing it, to be honest. I was just so lost in how much everything ached, but I was saying it out loud. Just over and over. She walked in, and I was just sitting there in the dark, saying out loud that I couldn't keep living like this. I was just repeating it to the empty room. \"I can't keep living like this, I can't keep living like this.\" Because it’s the truth, you know? I really feel like I can’t. It’s not even that I have some elaborate plan to do something violent to myself right this second, it’s just this overwhelming, desperate need for all of it to just stop. For me to just... stop being here. Because being here is just too hard.\n\nDoctor: How has your energy been during this period?\n\nPatient: My energy? It's... honestly, it's completely non-existent. It’s like the tank is just permanently on empty. I know I was just talking about how much time I spend in bed, and you'd think, logically, if someone is lying down for twelve or fourteen hours a day, they should have some kind of energy stored up, right? But it's the exact opposite for me. It's this deep, bone-weary exhaustion that just never, ever lifts. \n\nEven after I’ve been in bed half the day, just trying to do the most basic, everyday things... it’s monumental. Like taking a shower, for instance. I'll sit on the edge of the mattress for maybe forty-five minutes just staring at the bathroom door, trying to talk myself into standing up. And when I finally do, and I get under the water, I usually can't even stand there. I just sit on the shower floor because my legs feel like they're made of wet sand. And by the time I get out and manage to put on a pair of sweatpants, or... or even if I'm just trying to answer one single text message on my phone... it literally feels like I’ve already worked a full, grueling shift at a heavy physical job. \n\nI'll look at the screen, and maybe it's just a message from that friend I mentioned, just checking in, and just the thought of moving my thumbs to type out \"I'm okay\" takes more energy than I have in my entire body. My arms feel so heavy, and my brain feels like it's wading through thick mud. I just... I really don't understand how normal people just get up and do things. Make breakfast, go to the store, brush their teeth. Every single movement I make feels like I'm dragging a hundred pounds of weight behind me. So I just end up doing nothing. I just sink right back into the pillows because the fatigue is just so absolute. It doesn't matter how long I rest, I'm just always, always so completely drained.\n\nDoctor: How has your ability to concentrate been during this period?\n\nPatient: Concentrate? Um... I'm not really sure, to be honest. It's... it's hard to say because, well, like I was just saying about having absolutely no energy, I'm not exactly doing anything that requires me to focus, you know? \n\nI don't have a job right now, so it's not like I'm sitting at a desk trying to force myself to stare at spreadsheets or write reports or anything like that. And I'm not in school anymore—I took some college classes a while back, and I remember having to really focus to get through the reading assignments then, but now? I don't even try to read. I have all these books on my nightstand, these mystery novels I used to just devour in a single weekend, and they've just been gathering dust for I don't even know how long. So I couldn't tell you if I can concentrate on a book, because the thought of even opening one and looking at the words is just... it's too much. \n\nEven with the TV... sometimes my friend, the one who has the key, she'll come over and turn the TV on in the living room just to get some noise in the apartment. She'll put on those home renovation shows or baking competitions, and I'll be sitting right there on the couch facing the screen, but I'm not really watching it. I'm not following what the judges are saying or who is winning or what they're building. I'm just sort of staring blankly at the colors moving around. \n\nSo, I haven't really noticed if my concentration is bad or anything, just because I'm not actually testing it. I'm not doing puzzles or holding deep conversations. My brain is just kind of... blank. I'm just sitting there in the quiet most of the day, not really thinking about anything specific, just feeling that heaviness I told you about. So, I don't know. I'll just pace from the kitchen to the window until my legs ache so badly I can barely stand, but I just can't seem to stop the movement. It's like my motor is running on panic, even though I'm completely exhausted. \n\nBut then... then there are the other times, which honestly happens more often lately. It's exactly what you said about moving slowly. It's like gravity suddenly gets turned up to a hundred in my apartment. My friend came over last week and she watched me try to make a cup of coffee. Just a simple cup of coffee, right? You put the grounds in, you pour the water. But I was moving so incredibly slowly. Every time I tried to lift my arm, it literally felt like I was lifting wet cement. Just reaching for the mug... it took me what felt like five whole minutes just to pull the cabinet door open and get the mug down to the counter. \n\nShe actually had to step in and take the coffee pot out of my hands because I was just standing there in the middle of the kitchen moving in slow motion, like I was trapped underwater. My brain was telling my hand to move, to just pour the water, but the message just wasn't getting there, or it was just taking forever to travel down my arm. Like taking a shower, for instance. I'll sit on the edge of the mattress for maybe forty-five minutes just staring at the bathroom door, trying to talk myself into standing up. And when I finally do, and I get under the water, I usually can't even stand there. I just sit on the shower floor because my legs feel like they're made of wet sand. And by the time I get out and manage to put on a pair of sweatpants, or... or even if I'm just trying to answer one single text message on my phone... it literally feels like I’ve already worked a full, grueling shift at a heavy physical job. \n\nI'll look at the screen, and maybe it's just a message from that friend I mentioned, just checking in, and just the thought of moving my thumbs to type out \"I'm okay\" takes more energy than I have in my entire body. My arms feel so heavy, and my brain feels like it's wading through thick mud. I just... I really don't understand how normal people just get up and do things. Make breakfast, go to the store, brush their teeth. Every single movement I make feels like I'm dragging a hundred pounds of weight behind me. So I just end up doing nothing. I just sink right back into the pillows because the fatigue is just so absolute. It doesn't matter how long I rest, I'm just always, always so completely drained.\n\nDoctor: How has your ability to concentrate been during this period?\n\nPatient: Concentrate? Um... I'm not really sure, to be honest. It's... it's hard to say because, well, like I was just saying about having absolutely no energy, I'm not exactly doing anything that requires me to focus, you know? \n\nI don't have a job right now, so it's not like I'm sitting at a desk trying to force myself to stare at spreadsheets or write reports or anything like that. And I'm not in school anymore—I took some college classes a while back, and I remember having to really focus to get through the reading assignments then, but now? I don't even try to read. I have all these books on my nightstand, these mystery novels I used to just devour in a single weekend, and they've just been gathering dust for I don't even know how long. So I couldn't tell you if I can concentrate on a book, because the thought of even opening one and looking at the words is just... it's too much. \n\nEven with the TV... sometimes my friend, the one who has the key, she'll come over and turn the TV on in the living room just to get some noise in the apartment. She'll put on those home renovation shows or baking competitions, and I'll be sitting right there on the couch facing the screen, but I'm not really watching it. I'm not following what the judges are saying or who is winning or what they're building. I'm just sort of staring blankly at the colors moving around. \n\nSo, I haven't really noticed if my concentration is bad or anything, just because I'm not actually testing it. I'm not doing puzzles or holding deep conversations. My brain is just kind of... blank. I'm just sitting there in the quiet most of the day, not really thinking about anything specific, just feeling that heaviness I told you about. So, I don't know. I guess I just don't have a good answer for that one, since there's really nothing in my life right now to focus on anyway.\n\nDoctor: Have other people noticed that you move or speak much more slowly than usual, or that you seem unusually restless?\n\nPatient: Oh, um... yes, actually. It's... it's strange because it goes back and forth, and my friend, the one I keep mentioning, she definitely notices it. She comments on it, actually, because it can be so extreme. \n\nSome days... some days I just can't stay still. It's not even like I have energy, like I was saying before with the fatigue, it's more like this terrible, uncomfortable buzzing just trapped right under my skin. I'll just pace. I have this specific path in my apartment, from the little galley kitchen, past the edge of the couch, right to the front window in the living room. And I'll just walk that same line, over and over and over. I don't even know I'm doing it half the time until I realize that I'm staring out the window at nothing, and I've been doing it for hours. I'll just pace from the kitchen to the window until my legs ache so badly I can barely stand, but I just can't seem to stop the movement. It's like my motor is running on panic, even though I'm completely exhausted. \n\nBut then... then there are the other times, which honestly happens more often lately. It's exactly what you said about moving slowly. It's like gravity suddenly gets turned up to a hundred in my apartment. My friend came over last week and she watched me try to make a cup of coffee. Just a simple cup of coffee, right? You put the grounds in, you pour the water. But I was moving so incredibly slowly. Every time I tried to lift my arm, it literally felt like I was lifting wet cement. Just reaching for the mug... it took me what felt like five whole minutes just to pull the cabinet door open and get the mug down to the counter. \n\nShe actually had to step in and take the coffee pot out of my hands because I was just standing there in the middle of the kitchen moving in slow motion, like I was trapped underwater. My brain was telling my hand to move, to just pour the water, but the message just wasn't getting there, or it was just taking forever to travel down my arm. I can't force my body to go any faster. It's either pacing a hole into the floor until I'm in physical pain, or that wet cement feeling. There's just no normal in between anymore.\n\n[Last Question]\nHas this continuous depressed mood now lasted at least two full years?\n\n[Patient Info]\n- demographics: Female / 34 / not_in_workforce / some_college\n- severity: Severe\n\n[Targeted Info]\nname: Depressive Episode Duration\nmanifestation: \"The depression became constant around the following summer, and it has lasted about 1 year and 4 months.\"\n\n[Conversation Style]\nYou are a Verbose patient.\nA verbose patient may\n1) provide excessively detailed responses when describing their symptoms and experiences, even when the details are directly relevant,\n2) elaborate extensively on the background, events, thoughts, feelings, and personal experiences surrounding their symptoms, and\n3) demonstrate difficulty allowing the doctor to guide the interview or move on to another clinical topic.\nThis is how you are, not a performance you put on in every reply — let it show as much or as little as the moment calls for.\n\nWhat to do:\n- Answer the doctor's last question with what [Targeted Info] holds, in your own words.\n- You are speaking out loud, not reading a line you prepared.   },
  {
    "role": "user",
    "content": "Reply to the doctor's last question now, as the patient."

========== OUTPUT [patient] ==========
Two years? Um... no, it hasn't been quite that long. It feels like a lifetime, honestly, because when you're stuck in this kind of heavy, wet-cement feeling every single day, time just sort of stretches out and loses all its meaning. You just wake up and it's the same exact gray day as yesterday. But if I really sit and try to trace it back, try to look at an actual calendar in my head... it wasn't two full years ago. 

It was actually around the summer of last year when it just... stopped lifting. Before that, I'd have bad days, or maybe a bad week where I felt really low, but then there would be some kind of tiny break, you know? A day where I could at least manage to leave the apartment or feel somewhat okay. But then that summer hit, and I remember the heat was just so oppressive outside, and I just closed the blinds in the living room one afternoon and I never really opened them again. That was when it became completely constant. It just settled over me like this suffocating, heavy blanket that hasn't come off for a single second since. 

So, if I count it out from that summer until now... it's been about a year and four months. A year and four months of just... nothing. Just sinking into the mattress and pacing that same line on the floor and feeling completely, totally empty. My friend actually brought it up a few months ago when we hit the one-year mark. She was trying to get me to just go sit out on the tiny balcony because it was summer again, and she said, "You know you've been trapped in this since last summer, right?" And I just... I couldn't even process it. The fact that a whole year had passed while I was just staring at the wall. So yeah, it's been about a year and four months of it being this non-stop, every single day kind of misery. Not two years, but... it's still so long.

    "content": "Candidate Diseases: \n[\"F32.2\"]\n\nInterview Transcript: \nDoctor: Hello, welcome. What brings you in today?\n\nPatient: Well, I... I guess I’m here because I just don’t care about anything anymore. And I mean really, truly anything. It’s like this heavy, blank feeling where everything I used to love is just... gone. I used to be so active, you know? I was taking these art classes—I actually did some college for design years ago, so I always loved painting and sketching—but now the supplies are just sitting in the corner collecting dust and I can't even bring myself to look at them. \n\nAnd it’s not just the art. I used to go to these weekly board-game nights with a group of people, and we’d be up for hours, but now the thought of even texting them back to say I can't make it just feels impossible. I don't go hiking anymore, even though the trails are beautiful right now and I used to go every single weekend. I even had this whole plan to get back into shape and join my old recreational soccer team... but I don't care about that either. None of it matters. I just... I just sit in my room all day, by myself, and I watch the hours go by while my whole life just slips away from me. I don't know how to make myself care again.\n\nDoctor: How long have you been feeling this way?\n\nPatient: Well, it's... it's been a really long time. Honestly, it feels like an eternity when you're stuck in it, you know? But if I really think about it, it started shortly after the divorce. That was... gosh, it's been about a year and eleven months now. Almost two whole years of just... this. \n\nI remember when the divorce was finalized, everyone kept telling me, \"Oh, you'll bounce back, you just need time,\" and I thought maybe they were right, maybe I just needed to process it. But then the weeks turned into months, and the months just kept piling up. I kept waiting for that moment where the fog would lift and I'd want to pick up a paintbrush again or call my friends, but it just never happened. Instead, it just got heavier and heavier. \n\nLike, I can picture exactly what the weather was like when it really hit me that this wasn't just a phase. It was raining, and I was just sitting on my couch, looking at the boxes I hadn't unpacked yet from moving into this smaller place, and I realized I didn't care if they ever got unpacked. And that was, yeah, almost two years ago. One year and eleven months of just watching the dust settle on everything I used to be. It's just exhausting, having this much time pass and feeling like you haven't moved an inch.\n\nDoctor: During these nearly two years, have you had any period lasting two months or more when you felt essentially back to your usual self?\n\nPatient: No, no, I haven't. Not even close. I mean, two whole months of feeling normal? God, I wish. I really wish. Sometimes I'll have a day, or maybe just an afternoon, where I think, \"Okay, maybe I can do this. Maybe I can get up and put away the laundry or actually cook something instead of eating cereal.\" And I might even do it. I might fold a few shirts. But then... it's like the air just leaks out of me again. And even then, during those few hours, I wouldn't say I feel like my *usual* self. I just feel... slightly less dead, I guess? \n\nBut a period of two months? Absolutely not. It's just been this one, long, continuous stretch of gray. I keep thinking about how people say time heals all wounds, but it really hasn't healed anything for me. It's just stretched it out. Like, I remember around Christmas last year, my sister invited me over, and she was so excited, she had this whole big dinner planned, and I thought, \"Okay, this is it, the holidays, I'll get into the spirit, I'll snap out of it.\" But I sat there at her dining table, surrounded by my family, and I just felt completely hollow. I couldn't even pretend to smile when my niece opened her presents. And that was supposed to be a good time, you know? But there hasn't been any break in it. No two months, no one month, not even a full week where I could say, \"Yes, I'm back.\" It's just been this relentless weight, every single day, for a year and eleven months. Just... heavy.\n\nDoctor: On most days, do you feel sad, down, or depressed for much of the day?\n\nPatient: Yes. Yes, absolutely. It's... it's almost every single day. Most days I wake up and before I even open my eyes, I can feel it. It's this heavy, hollow feeling right in the center of my chest, just sitting there. It feels like... like there’s no future for me beyond the apartment walls, you know? Like the whole world outside my window is just happening without me, and I'm trapped in this little box, and I can't even find a reason to want to leave it. \n\nI'll just lie there in bed for hours, staring at the ceiling fan, watching the dust on the blades and thinking about how I should clean it, but I just feel so incredibly sad. It's not even a crying kind of sad most of the time, though sometimes I do cry, but it's more like... like a deep ache. A sinking feeling that just drags me down into the mattress. And it stays with me all day. I'll drag myself to the kitchen to make tea, and the sadness is just there, standing next to me while the kettle boils. I'll sit on the couch, and it's sitting right there with me. It just colors everything. The whole day is just... down. Just so, so down. I can't remember the last time I woke up and actually looked forward to the day, or felt light, or felt anything other than this crushing, suffocating sadness that just takes up the whole day, from morning until I finally manage to fall back asleep at night.\n\nDoctor: How has your sleep been during this period?\n\nPatient: Oh, it's... it's just been a nightmare. Honestly, sleep is supposed to be the one escape from all of this, right? The one time you don't have to feel that heaviness. But for me, it's almost worse than being awake during the day. \n\nI'll get into bed, usually pretty early because I'm just so exhausted from existing all day, but the minute the lights go out, it's like... I don't know, I just can't shut down. I end up lying awake for hours and hours. I'll just stare at the shadows on the ceiling, and the apartment gets so quiet, but not a peaceful quiet. It's like a loud quiet, if that makes sense? I just lie there, perfectly still, and I find myself just... listening. Straining my ears for every little creak in the floorboards or the sound of the wind against the glass. I get so on edge that I have to get up. I'll get out of bed, walk down the little hallway, and check the front door to make sure the deadbolt is turned. And then I'll go back to bed, but ten minutes later, I'm listening again, and I convince myself I didn't check it right, so I get up and check the door again. I just do that for hours, lying awake, listening, getting up, checking. \n\nAnd then... gosh, when my body finally just gives out from sheer exhaustion and I actually do fall asleep, it barely even counts as sleep. It never lasts. I always, always wake up way before dawn. Like, 3:30 or 4:00 in the morning. And I don't just wake up normally, I wake up and I'm just... shaking. My whole body will be trembling under the blankets, almost like I'm freezing cold, but I'm not. It's just this horrible, physical shaking, and my chest feels tight all over again. And once my eyes open and that shaking starts, that's it for the night. I try so hard to get back to sleep. I'll pull the comforter all the way up over my ears, I'll try counting backward, I'll try changing positions, but I just can't get back under. My brain is just awake, and my body is shivering in the dark, and I just have to lie there for another three hours waiting for the sun to come up, knowing I have to face another whole day feeling completely drained. It's just a vicious cycle.\n\nDoctor: Have you had thoughts that you would be better off dead, or thoughts of harming or killing yourself?\n\nPatient: Yes... yes, I have. I mean, especially when I was talking about the sleep just now? The only thing I can think about when I finally do close my eyes, when the exhaustion finally takes over, is... I just keep having these thoughts that I don't want to wake up anymore. It’s just this constant, heavy loop in my head. Like, I'll be lying there shivering, and I just think, \"Please, just let me stay asleep this time. Let it just be over.\" Because waking up just means doing the whole day again, carrying all that weight again, and I just... I can't. \n\nIt actually... it got to a point where it really scared my friend recently. She, um, she has a spare key to my apartment because she knows I don't always answer my phone, especially lately. I just can't bring myself to look at the screen, let alone talk to anyone. So I hadn't texted her back in maybe four or five days, I can't really remember, the days just kind of bleed into each other. But she got worried and she let herself in. I think it was a Tuesday afternoon. The blinds were all still drawn, the apartment was completely dark, and I was just sitting on the floor in the bedroom, right next to the bed. \n\nAnd when she found me... she told me later that she heard me before she even opened the bedroom door. I didn't even realize I was doing it, to be honest. I was just so lost in how much everything ached, but I was saying it out loud. Just over and over. She walked in, and I was just sitting there in the dark, saying out loud that I couldn't keep living like this. I was just repeating it to the empty room. \"I can't keep living like this, I can't keep living like this.\" Because it’s the truth, you know? I really feel like I can’t. It’s not even that I have some elaborate plan to do something violent to myself right this second, it’s just this overwhelming, desperate need for all of it to just stop. For me to just... stop being here. Because being here is just too hard.\n\nDoctor: How has your energy been during this period?\n\nPatient: My energy? It's... honestly, it's completely non-existent. It’s like the tank is just permanently on empty. I know I was just talking about how much time I spend in bed, and you'd think, logically, if someone is lying down for twelve or fourteen hours a day, they should have some kind of energy stored up, right? But it's the exact opposite for me. It's this deep, bone-weary exhaustion that just never, ever lifts. \n\nEven after I’ve been in bed half the day, just trying to do the most basic, everyday things... it’s monumental. Like taking a shower, for instance. I'll sit on the edge of the mattress for maybe forty-five minutes just staring at the bathroom door, trying to talk myself into standing up. And when I finally do, and I get under the water, I usually can't even stand there. I just sit on the shower floor because my legs feel like they're made of wet sand. And by the time I get out and manage to put on a pair of sweatpants, or... or even if I'm just trying to answer one single text message on my phone... it literally feels like I’ve already worked a full, grueling shift at a heavy physical job. \n\nI'll look at the screen, and maybe it's just a message from that friend I mentioned, just checking in, and just the thought of moving my thumbs to type out \"I'm okay\" takes more energy than I have in my entire body. My arms feel so heavy, and my brain feels like it's wading through thick mud. I just... I really don't understand how normal people just get up and do things. Make breakfast, go to the store, brush their teeth. Every single movement I make feels like I'm dragging a hundred pounds of weight behind me. So I just end up doing nothing. I just sink right back into the pillows because the fatigue is just so absolute. It doesn't matter how long I rest, I'm just always, always so completely drained.\n\nDoctor: How has your ability to concentrate been during this period?\n\nPatient: Concentrate? Um... I'm not really sure, to be honest. It's... it's hard to say because, well, like I was just saying about having absolutely no energy, I'm not exactly doing anything that requires me to focus, you know? \n\nI don't have a job right now, so it's not like I'm sitting at a desk trying to force myself to stare at spreadsheets or write reports or anything like that. And I'm not in school anymore—I took some college classes a while back, and I remember having to really focus to get through the reading assignments then, but now? I don't even try to read. I have all these books on my nightstand, these mystery novels I used to just devour in a single weekend, and they've just been gathering dust for I don't even know how long. So I couldn't tell you if I can concentrate on a book, because the thought of even opening one and looking at the words is just... it's too much. \n\nEven with the TV... sometimes my friend, the one who has the key, she'll come over and turn the TV on in the living room just to get some noise in the apartment. She'll put on those home renovation shows or baking competitions, and I'll be sitting right there on the couch facing the screen, but I'm not really watching it. I'm not following what the judges are saying or who is winning or what they're building. I'm just sort of staring blankly at the colors moving around. \n\nSo, I haven't really noticed if my concentration is bad or anything, just because I'm not actually testing it. I'm not doing puzzles or holding deep conversations. My brain is just kind of... blank. I'm just sitting there in the quiet most of the day, not really thinking about anything specific, just feeling that heaviness I told you about. So, I don't know. I guess I just don't have a good answer for that one, since there's really nothing in my life right now to focus on anyway.\n\nDoctor: Have other people noticed that you move or speak much more slowly than usual, or that you seem unusually restless?\n\nPatient: Oh, um... yes, actually. It's... it's strange because it goes back and forth, and my friend, the one I keep mentioning, she definitely notices it. She comments on it, actually, because it can be so extreme. \n\nSome days... some days I just can't stay still. It's not even like I have energy, like I was saying before with the fatigue, it's more like this terrible, uncomfortable buzzing just trapped right under my skin. I'll just pace. I have this specific path in my apartment, from the little galley kitchen, past the edge of the couch, right to the front window in the living room. And I'll just walk that same line, over and over and over. I don't even know I'm doing it half the time until I realize that I'm staring out the window at nothing, and I've been doing it for hours. I'll just pace from the kitchen to the window until my legs ache so badly I can barely stand, but I just can't seem to stop the movement. It's like my motor is running on panic, even though I'm completely exhausted. \n\nBut then... then there are the other times, which honestly happens more often lately. It's exactly what you said about moving slowly. It's like gravity suddenly gets turned up to a hundred in my apartment. My friend came over last week and she watched me try to make a cup of coffee. Just a simple cup of coffee, right? You put the grounds in, you pour the water. But I was moving so incredibly slowly. Every time I tried to lift my arm, it literally felt like I was lifting wet cement. Just reaching for the mug... it took me what felt like five whole minutes just to pull the cabinet door open and get the mug down to the counter. \n\nShe actually had to step in and take the coffee pot out of my hands because I was just standing there in the middle of the kitchen moving in slow motion, like I was trapped underwater. My brain was telling my hand to move, to just pour the water, but the message just wasn't getting there, or it was just taking forever to travel down my arm. It's so frustrating because I know I'm moving like that, I can feel how sluggish and impossibly heavy I am, and I know she's watching me do it, but I can't speed it up. I just... I can't force my body to go any faster. It's either pacing a hole into the floor until I'm in physical pain, or that wet cement feeling. There's just no normal in between anymore.\n\nDoctor: Has this continuous depressed mood now lasted at least two full years?\n\nPatient: Two years? Um... no, it hasn't been quite that long. It feels like a lifetime, honestly, because when you're stuck in this kind of heavy, wet-cement feeling every single day, time just sort of stretches out and loses all its meaning. You just wake up and it's the same exact gray day as yesterday. But if I really sit and try to trace it back, try to look at an actual calendar in my head... it wasn't two full years ago. \n\nIt was actually around the summer of last year when it just... stopped lifting. Before that, I'd have bad days, or maybe a bad week where I felt really low, but then there would be some kind of tiny break, you know? A day where I could at least manage to leave the apartment or feel somewhat okay. But then that summer hit, and I remember the heat was just so oppressive outside, and I just closed the blinds in the living room one afternoon and I never really opened them again. That was when it became completely constant. It just settled over me like this suffocating, heavy blanket that hasn't come off for a single second since. \n\nSo, if I count it out from that summer until now... it's been about a year and four months. A year and four months of just... nothing. Just sinking into the mattress and pacing that same line on the floor and feeling completely, totally empty. My friend actually brought it up a few months ago when we hit the one-year mark. She was trying to get me to just go sit out on the tiny balcony because it was summer again, and she said, \"You know you've been trapped in this since last summer, right?\" And I just... I couldn't even process it. The fact that a whole year had passed while I was just staring at the wall. So yeah, it's been about a year and four months of it being this non-stop, every single day kind of misery. Not two years, but... it's still so long."
